V, 23); but the locks are the innermost thoughts of the saints, which do not flow loose and unbound, but are gathered together by the bond of the fear and the love of God. But the dew and the drops of the nights in this place designate minds that are dark and full of unbelief and cold. The head, therefore, of the bridegroom is full of dew, when all worldly men grow torpid toward the charity of God, and according to what the Lord says: When sin aboundeth, charity groweth cold (Matth. XXIV, 12). And when such men persecute and hate the saints of God who meditate upon heavenly things, then as it were the locks of the bridegroom are full of the drops of the nights. When, therefore, such men are multiplied and burden the Church, then the bridegroom admonishes his bride that she rise and press on to the work of preaching.

Verse 3. I have put off my garment, how shall I put it on? The Church, being called on by her bridegroom to the office of preaching, answers: I have put off my garment: how shall I put it on? I have stripped myself of the cares and occupations 1082Cof this world, without which the office of preaching is carried on either scarcely or not at all, and how can it come about that I should return again to those things which I have forsaken? For he who fits himself to take up the office of preaching ought also to provide temporal things for his subjects, which cannot be managed without great solicitude. But that the garment signifies the solicitudes and cares of the world, the Lord shows in the Gospel: And he that is on the housetop, let him not come down to take his garment (Matth. XXIV, 18); which is plainly to say: He who stands on the lofty citadel of contemplation, let him not come down to take his garment 521, that is, let him not come down to take up the cares of the world; that is, lest he be again entangled in the occupations of the world. The Church, therefore, or every holy soul, fears to be stripped of this garment, and, resting in the contemplation of her Maker, to be clothed again and taken up with worldly business.

I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them? I have washed my feet, that is, the actions with which I was never accustomed to touch the dust, 1082Dthat is, to do earthly things, I have washed with the worthy weeping of penance, so much so that now nothing pleases me to think upon but divine things. How shall I defile them? that is, how can it come about that I should return again to thinking upon perishable and earthly things, I who now, in an occupied and free heart, enjoy the divine gaze?

Verse 4. My beloved put his hand through the hole, and my belly trembled at his touch. The beloved puts his hand through the hole and touches the belly, when the Maker visits the heart with inward inspiration, and kindles it to the advancement of the virtues: or also when he calls us back to the memory of his works, that we may consider: that, when he was God, he deigned to become man for us; that, taking on our earthly things, he might make us heavenly, and by dying for us might bestow eternal life upon us. And when this comes to pass, the belly trembles: because, when we begin to think upon such things, the secret places of our heart are shaken, while we begin to be amazed with how great a condescension the Maker deigned to do these things for us 1083AFor that the belly signifies the heart, the prophet shows, saying: My belly, my belly is in pain (Jer. IV, 19); and he shows what this was, saying: The senses of my heart are troubled.

Verse 5. I arose up to open to my beloved. We open to the beloved not only when we receive his coming, but also when we preach to others; and those who through malice had closed their breast, converted by our preaching, we bring it about that they too open the door of their heart to Christ. And well is it first said, I arose up; then, to open to my beloved. For he who by preaching wishes to open the hearts of others to Christ ought first to rise up, that is, to be lifted up to the zeal of good works, and to fulfill in work what he preaches: lest perhaps, preaching to others, he himself become reprobate (I Cor. IX, 27); hence also Luke says of the Lord: Which Jesus began to do and to teach (Act. I, 1); first he says to do, then to teach.

My hands dropped with myrrh, and my fingers 1083Bwere full of the choicest myrrh. By the hands with which we work is designated the working itself; but by the fingers, the discernment of the working: because in no member are the distinctions of the joints so many as in the fingers. The hands of the Church drop myrrh, when her preachers give their labor to continence and to the mortification of their flesh, saying with the Apostle: I chastise my body, and bring it into subjection (II Cor. IX, 27). Or when they are ready to die for Christ; as John says: If Christ laid down his life for us, we also ought to lay down our lives for the brethren (I Joan. III, 16). But the fingers are full of the choicest myrrh, when we subtly discern the work of mortification itself, whether it be done for the sake of the reward above, or for the praises of the world. For he who fasts for human favors, or presses on with works of abstinence or of continence, has indeed myrrh in his hands, but has it not in his fingers: because he does not discern why he does this; of whom the Lord says: Amen I say to you, they have received their reward (Matth. VI, 2). Or the hands of the bride drop 1083Cmyrrh, when they fit themselves to the works of the holy Church, of chastity or of holy continence.

Verse 6. I opened the bolt of my door to my beloved, but he had turned aside and passed on. She opens the bolt of the door when the Church, or any faithful soul, spreads open the heart to the Creator as he comes. But he had turned aside and passed on. For very often, when we wish to treat of things spiritual, the more subtly we strain, the more the eye of the heart is beaten back; whence Solomon says: I said, I shall be made wise, and she withdrew farther from me (Eccl. VII, 24); the Psalmist too says: Man shall come to a deep heart, and God shall be exalted (Psal. LXIII, 8). For the more anyone lifts up his heart to divine contemplation by the purifying of the flesh, the higher he finds that to be which he was seeking. He says therefore: He had turned aside and passed on, because no one can grasp him in this present life as he is.

My soul melted when the beloved spoke. The more nearly (she says) I perceived the coming of my bridegroom at the touch of his secret inbreathing, the more did 1083Dwhatever was cold in me grow warm, so that now nothing pleases me but to be dissolved into tears. For the bridegroom speaks when Christ breathes into the holy soul by his inward inspiration, and suggests to it a heavenly desire; and so, as he speaks, it melts: because the more it is breathed upon by heavenly desires, the more fully it dies to earthly things and is made as it were insensible to the world, and lives by heavenly desire alone. This is what is also said through the prophet: Who is blind but my servant? Who is deaf, but he to whom I have sent my messengers? (Isai. XLII, 19).

I sought him, and found him not: I called, and he answered me not. Again she redoubles the cause of her grief. To all indeed who seek him devoutly the Lord is always at hand: to those who call upon him he answers, that is, he hears them unto salvation; but very often he does not hear the faithful soul that desires him and longs to come to him, to this end, that he may so show himself to it as present as he promises he will show himself in the time to come; 1084Aor also he does not answer, that is, he does not hear, to this end, that at his own good pleasure every holy man may be stripped of the bonds of the body and, being free, be caressed by the embraces of his bridegroom. For when the Apostle said: I desire to be dissolved and to be with Christ (Philip. I, 23), he was as it were seeking to cleave to his bridegroom, and calling him with the desire of his mind; and yet he did not answer him, because he did not at once free him from the body, but permitted him still to be exercised with labors and to sweat over the needs of the Church.

Verse 7. The keepers who go about the city found me; they struck me and wounded me. The watchmen who guard the city are the holy teachers, who go about the city of God (that is, the Church), keeping unsleeping care of her, that they may both defend her from adversaries and, by their word or their example, kindle her to heavenly desires. They find the bride, therefore, seeking her beloved, when the holy teachers instruct and form the soul that is intent upon heavenly desires — present, by their word, or 1084Babsent, by their sacred writings. They struck me (she says) and wounded me. The word of God is a sword, as the Apostle says: The word of God is living and effectual, and more piercing than any two-edged sword, and reaching unto the division of the soul and the spirit (Hebr. IV, 12). By this sword the bride is struck and wounded, when the preacher's discourse speaks of God, and pierces the hearers through as it were with a certain dart of compunction and love.

522 The keepers of the walls took my cloak from me. This cloak here is understood as the tunic above, where it is said: I have put off my tunic — namely, the covering of worldly desires, which the keepers take away: because whatever of the love of this present world has remained in anyone's mind, the holy teachers take it all away, and make her love only her bridegroom Christ. Such as these can say with the Apostle: The world is crucified to me, and I to the world (Gal. VI, 14). The keepers of the walls. The walls of the city are the holy teachers of the Church. But the keepers of the walls are those 1084Cwho instruct even those who are able to preside over others, such as Paul was, who instructed and guarded Timothy and Titus — walls, surely, of this city — that is, exhorted them to the perfection of good work.

Verse 8. I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my beloved, that you tell him that I languish with love. The daughters of Jerusalem are the faithful souls, whether they are still sojourning in this present life, or already enjoy Christ, or are one day to contemplate Christ face to face. Or the daughters of Jerusalem are the citizens of that city above, in which is the highest peace and the happiness of divine contemplation. But in this place we ought to take the daughters of Jerusalem as souls that desire God, but are still passing their time in this pilgrimage. The bride therefore adjures the daughters of Jerusalem to tell the bridegroom that she languishes with love, when the faithful soul adjures other faithful souls that desire God to make it known to Christ, and to tell him that she is held by his love. And well does she say that she languishes with love. For the more she has begun to desire the things above, 1084Dthe more she grows faint toward the things that are of the world. Nor is it any wonder if such are said to languish, since the Apostle calls them dead: You are dead, he says, and your life is hid with Christ (Coloss. III, 3). So then the daughters of Jerusalem answer the bride who adjures them.

Verse 9. What manner of one is thy beloved of the beloved, O most beautiful of women? what manner of one is thy beloved of the beloved, that thou hast so adjured us? This comes to pass when some faithful man addresses a faithful brother, and they suggest to each other words of heavenly desires, by which they kindle themselves to the love of things above. Tell me, she says, who now desire, who now long to come to the vision of my Creator, what manner of one is thy beloved, that is, in what manner Christ ought to be loved. Long since, she says, I began to fear him; but now, since charity drives out fear, I choose to hear words by which I may be kindled to his love. Do thou therefore, who already seekest him with love, 1085Aand languishest with love of him, tell me: What manner of one is thy beloved of the beloved. Beloved of the beloved — the Son is of the Father, as he is God of God, light of light. Or also, thy beloved of the beloved is Christ on that side by which he is to be loved, not on that by which he is to be feared. For to fear belongs rather to the imperfect, whom fear calls back from sins; but to love, to the perfect. Now therefore she asks What manner of one the beloved is of the beloved, that is, in what manner Christ ought to be loved, or of what sort he is toward those who have known how to love him only.

Verse 10. My beloved is white and ruddy, chosen out of thousands. The voice of the Church answering: My beloved is white and ruddy: white by virginity, ruddy by his passion. White, because without sin; for without sin did he pass his life: Sin indeed he did not do, neither was guile found in his mouth (I Petr. II, 22). Ruddy, because he washed us from our sins in his own blood (Apoc. I, 5). Chosen out of thousands. For by a singular grace Christ the man shone out in the whole 1085Bmass of the human race: because through him God purposed to save the human race, and he himself is the Mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus (I Tim. II, 5); of whom the Psalmist says: There is none that doth good, there is not so much as one (Psal. XIII, 3); and Solomon: One man among a thousand I have found (Eccle. VII, 29), that is, Christ; and he himself in the psalm: I am alone until I pass (Psal. CXL, 10). Chosen therefore out of thousands, because out of all the multitude of the saints he alone was worthy to hear: This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased (Matth. III, 17).

Verse 11. His head is the finest gold. The head of the bridegroom is God, according to what the Apostle says: The head of Christ is God (I Cor. XI, 3); which is compared to the finest gold, because as gold is more precious than all metals, so almighty God excels and rises above the good things made by him; whence the Lord says: None is good but God alone (Marc. X, 18). The head therefore of the bridegroom is the finest gold, because the goodness of God is unspeakable, transcending the goodness of all things: because nothing is good save by 1085Cparticipation in this highest good.

His locks are as the elatae of palm trees. The locks of the bridegroom are the multi udines of the faithful, cleaving to God through faith and love, which are compared to the elatae of palm trees. The elatae of palm trees are the longer and more excellent branches, at times of a golden color, curling and ever stretching toward the heights, and never losing their greenness. Such then does the Church call the locks of her bridegroom — curling, that is, and glowing red. Some say that the elatae of palm trees are a species of aromatic tree, which in Latin they call abies or spatae; for in Greek the fir is called ἐλάτη. And this species is fit for the making of ointments. But by abies here we ought to understand not that great tree which is fit for ships and houses, but (as we have said) a species of aromatic tree. So much, according to the letter, concerning the elatae of palm trees. The locks therefore of the bridegroom are compared to the elatae of palm trees: because the multitudes of the faithful both rejoice in the greenness of faith, and are lifted up to desire things eternal, and taste the sweetness of the delight 1085Dabove; and the more they perceive of it, the more do they seem in their own eyes sinners and contemptible; whence there follows:

Black as a raven. For the locks of the bridegroom are black as a raven, because the multitudes of the faithful are conscious to themselves of their own frailty, and have known that of themselves they have nothing good; or also, they are elatae of palm trees in hope and in victory, but black as a raven, as being despised and contemptible amid the pressures of this world. The elatae of palm trees are the saints, because by the grace of God they press on toward the palm of heavenly victory; but black as a raven, because they know themselves to be of themselves weak and sinners. The locks of the bridegroom may also be taken as the angelic powers, which cleave to God in a special way, as the locks to the head, and contemplate him from close at hand. And these are rightly compared to the elatae of palm trees: because they are never bent from the uprightness of their estate, but remain unstained in the dignity of their office; and the more they contemplate the brightness of their Creator, the 1086Amore fully do they behold, in comparison with him, how worthless is every created thing, and therefore they are said to be black as a raven.

523 Verse 12. His eyes are as doves upon brooks of waters, which are washed with milk. His eyes are the gifts of the Holy Spirit; which John shows in the Apocalypse, saying: I saw a Lamb as it were slain, having seven horns and seven eyes, which are the seven spirits of God sent into all the earth (Apoc. V, 6). Whence also they are well compared to doves upon brooks of waters, because the Holy Spirit is delighted with pure and sincere minds. For doves settle not upon pools or marshes, but upon brooks of waters: because (as has been said) the Holy Spirit makes chaste and clean minds a dwelling-place for himself. Which are washed with milk: because (as has been said) the Holy Spirit is understood under the name of milk, that is, the grace of God, which is bestowed upon the Church through the Holy Spirit, and by which, as little ones by milk, so the Church is nourished. We may also take by the eyes of the bridegroom the teachers of the Church, who are compared to doves 1086Bon account of their innocence and simplicity. These doves settle upon the brooks, that they may see the coming of the hawk from afar and be on their guard: so also the holy teachers, drenched with the water of baptism, are made white with the brightness of chastity and holiness.

And they settle beside the fullest streams, that is, beside the abounding knowledge of the Scriptures. But if we think there is any difference between the brooks and the fullest streams, it must be said that by the brooks are signified the writings of the Old Testament; by the fullest streams is taken the copious teaching of the holy Gospel. These doves therefore are both upon the brooks of waters, and settle beside the fullest streams: because the holy teachers assiduously search out perserutantur the mysteries of the Old Testament as well as of the New.

Verse 13. His cheeks are as beds of spices planted by the perfumers. By the cheeks of the bridegroom is taken the modest kindness of Christ, or his severity, or the bearing of his countenance; for in a man's cheeks gladness, or modesty, severity 1086Calso and kindness are discerned. And therefore in the cheeks of the bridegroom the bearing of our Redeemer's countenance is rightly taken. For those were his cheeks when he rejoiced in spirit, as the Gospel says, or when he wept over the dead Lazarus (Joan. XI, 35), or when he grieved over Jerusalem that was to perish (Luc. XIX, 41), and many things of that kind. These cheeks of the bridegroom are compared to beds of spices; for as beds of spices, excellently laid out and set in order, delight the sight of those who look on them, and offer them the grace of their fragrance: so the meekness of Christ delighted the absent by report and those present in very deed, and called them forth to love of him. These beds are planted by the perfumers. The perfumers are the prophets or the apostles, of whom the one sort described all these things as still to come, the other as already done.

His lips are lilies dropping the first myrrh. By the lips of the bridegroom are taken the words of our Lord Jesus Christ, which are compared to lilies: because they announce the rewards of eternal whiteness. They drop the first myrrh, because they teach that these things are to be reached 1086Dthrough the mortification of the flesh. Or also the lips of the Lord are compared to lilies, because they teach the works of chastity and cleanness. They drop the first myrrh, because they suggest that whatever adversities come are to be borne patiently. The lips of the Lord were lilies when, teaching on the mountain, he said: Blessed are the poor in spirit, blessed are the meek, blessed are the merciful, blessed are the clean of heart (Matth. V, 3, 4, 7, 8, 10). They drop the first myrrh, when after these things he added: Blessed are they that suffer persecution for justice' sake.

Verse 14. His hands are turned and golden. For in the hands of the bridegroom the working of the Saviour is worthily taken. For as a man works easily with the lathe, and needs no rule, so the working of the Saviour was easy; for whatever he willed, he accomplished on the spot: because he spoke, and they were made (Psal. XXXII, 9); and of whom the evangelist says (Matth. VII, 29): He was as one having his word in power. For they were crant golden, because his working was carried through by the power of the divinity; for in gold the Divinity 1087Ais figured: because as gold surpasses the other metals and is more precious, so the Divinity excels all its own works in an unspeakable manner.

Full of hyacinths. The hyacinth is a stone of the color of air. What then is taken in the hyacinths, but hope and heavenly desire? And the hands of the Lord are full of hyacinths, because all his working stirs us up to hope and desire of the things above. The hyacinth is also an aromatic flower, of purple color and of good scent. The hands of the Lord are full of hyacinths, because on the cross they were pierced with nails, and sprinkled with the redness of his own blood as with a purple color. But the former exposition, concerning the hyacinth as a stone, fits better here. In another translation a stone is found. For his hands are said to be turned, full of Tharsis. Tharsis is the stone which we call chalcedony.

His belly is of ivory. Among the members of the human body nothing is frailer than the belly, nothing more tender; and therefore by the belly is taken the frailty of the humanity assumed in Christ. Now ivory is the bone of the elephant, which animal 1087Bthey say is most chaste, and of a most cold nature whence also it is vehemently sought after by the dragon, which is of a hot nature. The belly therefore of the bridegroom is of ivory, because the assumed humanity of the Redeemer admitted no corruption, no stain; Sin indeed he did not do, neither was any guile found in him (Isai. LIII, 9). He assumed indeed the frailty of the flesh; and yet he was free of all sin; just as the brazen serpent lifted up by Moses in the desert (Num. XXI, 9) had indeed the figure of a serpent, but lacked the malice of venom.

Distinguished with sapphires. The sapphire is a stone having the color of a clear sky. Whence in the vision of the Lord it is said: And they saw the God of Israel, and under his feet as it were a work of sapphire stone, and as the heaven when it is clear (Exod. XXIV, 10). By the sapphires therefore are understood the works of the divine Majesty, which are accomplished in the flesh. The belly therefore of the bridegroom was distinguished with sapphires: because the humanity of Christ shone with heavenly powers. Nor is he said to be full of sapphires, but distinguished with them; so, 1087Cnamely, that between the sapphires the whiteness of the ivory might appear: because the Lord so worked those things that were of the divinity, that he none the less accomplished those that were of the humanity, and did not leave off those that were of the divinity. For the working in Christ, of the divinity and of the humanity, is distinguished. For that he hungered, that he thirsted, that he wept, that he grew weary, that at the last he could be crucified and die, were works of the humanity; but that he raised the dead, that he came to the aid of all the sick, that he raised himself from the dead, were most evident works of the divinity.

Verse 15. His Crara are pillars of marble, which are founded upon bases of gold. The legs of the bridegroom are the journeys of the Saviour, by which he deigned to become man and to come to us; and these are rightly compared to pillars of marble, because nothing is stronger than marble, nothing straighter than a pillar. And the legs of the bridegroom are pillars of marble: because all the ways of the Lord are both strong and straight. Of his strength 524 the Psalmist says: He rejoiced as a giant to run the way, his going forth is from the top of heaven 1087D(Psal. XV II, 6). Of his straightness, elsewhere: The Lord is just, and hath loved justices; his countenance hath beheld equity (Psal. X, 8); and, All the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth (Psal. XXIV, 10). These legs are founded upon bases of gold, because all things that were to be done through Christ or in Christ were foreordained and determined beforehand by the counsel of the divinity before the founding of the world. For gold, which (as has often been said) is more splendid and more precious than the other metals, signifies the most unalloyed secret of the divine counsel. From the bases of gold therefore the pillars of marble rise, because from the beginning of the world were foreordained the incarnation, the nativity, the passion and the resurrection of the Saviour, and the other mysteries by which the human race was to be redeemed. Whence also the prophet, praising God, said: O Lord my God, I will honor thee, I will sing praise to thy name, who doest wonderful things: let thy counsel be made true (Isai. XXV, 1).

His fairness is as of Lebanon, chosen as the cedars. Lebanon 1088Ais a mountain of Phoenicia, the boundary of Judea toward the north, and it is loftier than the other mountains. The trees also of that mountain excel the timber of all other forests both in height and in fairness and in strength. As (he says) Lebanon is loftier than the other mountains of the land of Judea: so our Redeemer is fairer than all the elect: because he is fair in form above the sons of men (Psal. XLIV, 3). And as the cedar is more beautiful and taller than the other trees, so he too by the grace of the divinity excels all the saints from the beginning of the world.

Verse 16. His throat is most sweet. Above, by the lips of the bridegroom his words were understood; now, by the throat, the inward sweetness of those very words is signified, by which our understanding is refreshed; whence also the Psalmist: Taste and see that the Lord is sweet (Psal. XXXIII, 9.) And many indeed can easily understand the words of the Lord; but few are they who are able to penetrate their sweetness, that is, who are eager to fulfil the precepts of God out of love and desire.

And he is wholly desirable. As if he were to say: Why do I linger in describing him 1088Bthrough his single members? Wholly desirable is my bridegroom. Wholly (she says), because perfect God, perfect man. Wholly desirable therefore is he, because he kindles the angels and all the elect to desire of him, according to what the Apostle says: On whom the angels desire to look (I Petr. I, 12); and the more nearly he is beheld, the more does he provoke those who behold him to gaze upon him with greater desire.

Such is my beloved, and he is my friend, O daughters of Jerusalem. Beloved, because through faith and charity I cleave to him; but friend, because he has deigned to make me, redeemed from the bond of sin, his friend, and privy to his secrets; and the more anyone loves him, the more worthy of his friendship is he made.

Verse 17. Whither has thy beloved gone, O most beautiful of women? It is the voice of the daughters of Jerusalem, that is, of the Churches, or also of the holy souls. For the bride of Christ is the Church, and the daughters of Jerusalem are that same Church, which consists 1088Cof the several souls of the faithful. They say therefore: Whither has thy beloved gone, O most beautiful of women?

Whither has thy beloved turned aside? and we will seek him with thee. For the bride herself had above adjured the daughters of Jerusalem to tell the bridegroom that she languished with his love; those same daughters of Jerusalem had asked what manner of one the bridegroom was, and she accordingly had set forth the form of her bridegroom; now the daughters of Jerusalem themselves ask whither that bridegroom has gone, that they may seek him with her. This the holy souls do, when they converse with one another concerning eternal salvation: burning with the love of Christ, and desiring the sight of him; or when they diligently search out in whose hearts especially Christ may rest, that they may be built up by their example and manner of life. And it is to be noted what is said, Whither has he gone, or, Whither has thy beloved turned aside? For he as it were turns aside for a time while he is sought, that he may stir up a greater desire to seek him. For to this end he very often defers the vows and prayers of the saints, that they may begin to desire him more ardently; just as of old 1088Dhe did not at once show himself to Mary who sought him, but appearing in the form of a gardener (Joan. XX, 15), he showed at length that he himself was the one who was sought. Christ therefore always hears his elect, but he always as it were turns aside, when he does not at once grant what is asked. And well is it said: we will seek with thee; for he who seeks Christ without the Church can wander and grow weary, but cannot profit.

CAPUT VI.

Vers. 1. Dilectus meus descendit in hortum suum ad areolam aromatis, ut pascatur in hortis, et lilia colligat. Jam supra docti sumus quis sit hortus Christi, sancta videlicet Ecclesia, de qua superius dictum est: Hortus conclusus soror mea sponsa. Descendit (inquit) Christus in hortum suum ad areolam, quem ipse praeparavit et excoluit, quem virtutum germinibus consevit, quem sua gratia illustravit, quem 1089Amuro suae custodiae ab incursu malignorum spirituum defendit. Et bene in hortum suum descendisse dicitur; nam in horto sepeliri voluit (Joan. XIX, 41), et primum Mariae Magdalenae in horto apparuit (Joan. XX, 15), ibique quodammodo primitias Ecclesiae consecravit. Descendisse dicitur, quando de superioribus et coelestibus sedibus in hortum descendit, quem ipse humili loco plantavit, sed ad hoc ipse descendit, ut hortus (id est Ecclesia) ejus ad ipsum ascenderet. Unde et in sequentibus de horto, id est Ecclesia sua, dicit: Quae est ista quae ascendit per desertum? Ad areolam (inquit) aromatis. Per areolam, anima cujusque fidelis intelligitur, quae disciplina coelesti exculta et diligenter composita, ac laterum parilitate exquadrata est. Ut pascatur (inquit) in hortis, id est, ex piis sanctorum laboribus delectetur. Ipse enim bonis nostris pascitur, in tantum, ut in paupere ipse reficiatur. Et lilia (inquit) colligat, hoc est, ut sanctas animas virtutis maturitate ad perfectum candorem 1089Bperductas, ab hoc mundo ad se colligat, et secum in aeterna beatitudine gaudere faciat. Lilia enim sanctae sunt animae, virtutum studiis et bonis operibus candidatae.

Vers. 2. Ego dilecto meo, et dilectus meus mihi, qui pascitur inter lilia. Ego dilecto meo, subauditur, gratam pastionem praeparo; et dilectus meus mihi gratiam perfectionis, vel praemium aeternae remunerationis praestabit. Sive, Ego dilecto meo in me ipsa mansionem paro; et dilectus meus mihi, quia ipse in me habitat, et me in se habitare facit; sicut ipse in Evangelio dicit: Ego in vobis, et vos in me (Joan. XIV, 20).

Vers. 3. Pulchra es, amica mea, suavis et decora sicut Jerusalem. Quaerebat Ecclesia sponsum suum tanquam absentem; sed ille ut se semper praesentem 525 ostenderet, eique de se colloquenti, seque desideranti semper adesse demonstraret, cito respondet: Pulchra es, amica mea, suavis et decora sicut Jerusalem. Pulchra es, quia maculam et rugam non habes 1089Cpeccati, sed omni nitore virtutum vernas. Amica mea, Ecclesia vel anima quaeque fidelis. Suavis et decora sicut Jerusalem. Jerusalem, quae visio pacis interpretatur, illam supernam civitatem designat in qua est vera pax et felicitas: quia ibi sic gaudent sancti de munere; ut non sit quod doleant de corruptione. Ergo Ecclesia, quae adhuc in terris est, vel anima quaeque fidelis, ad similitudinem illius Jerusalem suavis est et decora: quia illam pacem in quantum potest imitatur. Sequitur:

Terribilis ut castrorum acies ordinata. Terribilis est Ecclesia malignis spiritibus, et est ordinata ut castrorum acies; cum videlicet unusquisque fidelis ordinem suum et vocationem suam recte custodit. Tres enim sunt ordines Ecclesiae, doctorum, continentium, et conjugatorum, quae species in tribus viris illis demonstratae sunt, Noe, Daniel et Job. Noe enim, qui inter fluctus arcam rexit, sanctos doctores significat; Daniel caelebs et castus, continentium ordinem significat; 1089DJob, qui uxorem habuit et filios, ordinem conjugatorum exprimit. Ergo Ecclesia ordinata est sicut acies castrorum, quia unusquisque ordo Ecclesiae velut in acie contra hostes malignorum spirituum consistit, dum sancti doctores praedicationi et doctrinae operam impendunt. Continentes quae mundi sunt contemnentes, tantum quae Dei sunt cogitant, et libera a terrenis occupationibus mente, soli Deo vacant. Conjugati eleemosynis et caeteris actualis vitae operibus bonis insistunt; sicque utuntur mundo, ut quae Dei sunt, non deserant. Haec ergo ordinatissima dispositio Ecclesiae terribilis est immundis spiritibus, velut acies castrorum ad bellum ordinata. Sive pulchra est Ecclesia et decora sicut Jerusalem in virtutibus: terribilis vero ut castrorum acies ordinata: quia imperium suum et potestatem proferre non cessat usque ad terminos totius orbis. Sive terribilis est ut castrorum acies ordinata: quia sic unitate charitatis connexa est et conjuncta, ut nulla vi maligni hostis, 1090Anulla peste discordiae possit penetrari; nihil enim sic terret malignos spiritus quomodo charitas.

Vers. 4. Averte oculos tuos a me, quia ipsi me avolare fecerunt. Ac si diceret: Ego quidem dedi tibi oculos columbinos, quibus me intueri et arcana Scripturarum acutissime penetrare valeres; sed cave ne illos ad me videndum modo intendere velis: quia dum in praesenti vivis, non potes; Non enim videbit me homo, et vivet (Exod. XXXIII, 20). Cum autem vinculis carnis absoluta ad me perveneris, tunc me aperta visione videbis, et implebitur quod in Evangelio promittitur: Qui autem diligit me, diligetur a Patre meo, et ego diligam eum, et manifestabo ei meipsum (Joan. XIV, 21). Quod autem dicitur, quia ipsi me avolare fecerunt, non est putandum quod Deus quaerentes se deserat, et ab illis recedat, cum ipse praecipiat: Quaerite et invenietis (Luc. XI, 9); sed ita potius accipiendum, quia quanto amplius quisque divinitatem majestatis Dei perscrutari voluerit, eo 1090Bamplius intelligit, quam imperscrutabilis atque incomprehensibilis sit. Et hoc est quod Psalmista dicit: Accedet homo ad cor altum, et exaltabitur Deus (Psal. LXIII, 7). Ac si diceret: Licet homo ad videndum Deum cor sublevet, exaltabitur Deus, quia quantus sit comprehendi non potest.

Capilli tui sicut grex caprarum quae apparuerunt de Galaad. Hi versiculi saepius expositi sunt; sed non pigeat iterato exponere, quos non piguit Spiritum sanctum repetere. Ideo enim bis istos versiculos posuit, ut certissimum et stabile teneremus, quod sub repetitione confirmari audiremus. Capilli Ecclesiae sanctae, multiplices sunt subtilium cogitationum varietates. Et bene comparantur gregi caprarum; nam capra peccatoris figura est; unde et in lege praecipitur, ut quicunque per ignorantiam peccaverit et cognoverit reatum suum, offerat pro delicto suo capram in holocaustum (Lev. IV, 28). Capilli ergo Ecclesiae gregi caprarum comparantur, quia sancti, qui sunt membra Ecclesiae, omnes reatus cogitationis suae per dignam 1090Cpoenitentiam et per lacrymas justae compunctionis delere satagunt. Bene autem de monte Galaad apparuisse dicuntur. Galaad interpretatur acervus testimonii: ideoque per Galaad mens sanctorum designatur, qui sunt acervus testimonii quoniam omnia quae saeculi sunt negligere, et solum Deum desiderare evidentissimis attestatur indiciis. Potest etiam per Galaad ipse Dominus et Redemptor noster intelligi. Galaad enim et montis et civitatis nomen est. De monte ergo Galaad grex caprarum apparuit, quia Ecclesia super Christum est fundata, sicut ipse dicit: Non potest civitas abscondi supra montem posita (Matth. V, 15). Ipse est acervus testimonii: quia multitudo prophetarum et patriarcharum gestis et scriptis ei testimonium perhibent.

Vers. 5. Dentes tui sicut greges ovium, quae ascenderunt de lavacro, omnes gemellis fetibus, et sterilis non est in eis. Per dentes Ecclesiae firma sermonum ejus stabilitas intelligitur. Qui bene gregi ovium comparantur quae de lavacro ascenderunt, quia verba Ecclesiae nihil nisi innocentiam et mansuetudinem 1090Dsonant, nihilque in se foedum, nihilque turpe vel immundum retinent; sed lavacro sinceritatis et puritatis lavantur, audientes ab Apostolo: Sermo malus ab ore vestro non procedat (Ephes. IV, 29). Bene autem omnes gemellis fetibus esse dicuntur, quia sermo sanctorum nihil aliud sonat nec loquitur, nisi quod ad dilectionem Dei et proximi pertinet. Solummodo enim ea sancti loqui debent, quae ad perpetuam corporum et animarum pertineant sanitatem.

Vers. 6. Sicut cortex mali punici, sic genae tuae absque occultis tuis. In genis diximus superius sanctorum castam verecundiam figurari; in cortice mali punici, mysterium passionis Christi, audientes a Paulo apostolo: Noli erubescere crucem Christi, neque me vinctum ejus (II Tim. I, 8). Et haec quidem magna sunt, quae in aperto sunt: maxima vero sunt illa quae sunt intrinsecus, hoc est, quae in futuro sanctis reservantur.

1091AVers. 7. Sexaginta sunt reginae, et octoginta concubinae, et adolescentularum non est numerus. Reginae sunt doctores sanctae Ecclesiae, qui merito fidei et scientiae thoro Regis aeterni propinquant, et spirituales Deo filios pariunt. Sexagenarius numerus ex denario et senario perficitur. Denarius enim per senarium multiplicatus 526 in sexagenarium consurgit. Et denario significantur divina praecepta propter Decalogum legis; senarius vero pro perfectione ponitur, quia sex diebus perfecit Deus opera sua. Reginae ergo sexaginta esse dicuntur, quia eos significant, ut diximus, qui perfectione scientiae et operis pollent, et propter solum amorem Conditoris sui, et intuitum supernae remunerationis praedicant. Hic etiam numerus, id est sexagenarius ex duodenario quinquies ducto conficitur; nam quinquies duodeni, vel duodecies quini sexaginta fiunt. Reginae ergo sexaginta esse dicuntur, quia illos significant qui in Ecclesia constituti, quinque sui corporis sensus juxta institutionem duodecim apostolorum caste 1091Bdisponere noverunt. Concubinae eos significant qui non sincere Christum praedicant, sed propter lucra temporalia, vel propter laudes populares. Nam et istae sicut reginae ad thorum Regis per scientiam accedunt, et filios spirituales generant; sed a corona aeterni regni alienae existunt. Et bene octogenario numero comprehenduntur; nam hic numerus ex denario et octonario conficitur. Octogenarius vero in malo accipitur aliquando propter quaternarium, quo multiplicato consistit. Quaternarius enim temporalia quaeque et praesentia significat propter quatuor mundi climata vel quatuor anni tempora. Recte ergo concubinae istae octoginta esse dicuntur: quia (ut dictum est) propter sola temporalia et visibilia praedicant, et spiritualia minus curant. De talibus Apostolus dicebat: Sunt quidam Christum annuntiantes non sincere, etc. (Philip. I, 17, 18); donec dicit: in hoc gaudeo, sed et gaudebo. Adolescentularum non est numerus. Adolescentulae sunt animae jam in Christo 1091Cregeneratae, quae deposita primi hominis vetustate in Christo renovatae sunt, necdum tamen sunt nubiles, necdum thoro Regis aptae; quia necdum ad perfectionem Christum praedicandi pervenerunt. Quarum non est numerus, quia infinitus est numerus animarum in Christo credentium, nec ab ullo homine comprehendi potest. Caeterum Deo numerati sunt omnes electi; nam apud quem etiam stellarum numerus comprehensus est (Psal. CXLVI, 4), ignorari non potest electorum numerus.

Vers. 8. Una est columba mea, perfecta mea. Hoc est, una est catholica per universum mundum diffusa Ecclesia, quae ex reginis et adolescentulis, sive adolescentibus constans, etiam concubinas habet, hoc est, quosdam qui nomine tenus Christiani vel doctores dicuntur. Una est ergo Ecclesia, quia scissuram et schismata non recipit; sed sicut unus est Deus, una fides, unum baptisma (Ephes. IV, 5), ita una est generalis Ecclesia, quae recte et columba vocatur: quia Spiritus sancti dote, qui in specie colombae apparuit, 1091DChristo sponsata et sanctificata est.

Una est matri suae, electa genitricis suae. Mater nostra illa est coelestis Jerusalem, de qua Apostolus dicit: Illa autem quae sursum est Jerusalem, libera est, quae est mater nostra. Haec ergo Ecclesia una est matri suae, quia ad exemplum illius Ecclesiae, quae jam Christo fruitur, ipsa in hac peregrinatione instituitur et informatur, atque ad illam beatitudinem in qua cum Christo conregnat, pervenire nititur.

Viderunt illam filiae Sion, et beatissimam praedicaverunt reginae, et concubinae laudaverunt eam. Quas superius adolescentulas, hic filias appellat. Reginas vero et concubinas in hoc loco, ut superius, intelligamus. Filiae ergo, reginae et concubinae praedicant Ecclesiam et laudant, quia universitas fidelium catholicam admiratur Ecclesiam. Ipsa enim Ecclesia, quae ex multis fidelium constat personis, catholicam, id est universalem Ecclesiam conficit.

1092AVers. 9. Quae est ista quae progreditur quasi aurora consurgens? Vox est Synagogae. In fine siquidem saeculi cum plebs Judaica ad praedicationem Eliae et Enoch conversa fuerit ad Christum, ipsa quoque Ecclesiam admirabitur, dicens: Quae est ista? id est, quanta et qualis est Ecclesia, quae progreditur? hoc est, proficit de virtute in virtutem. Non enim stare, sed progredi dicitur Ecclesia: quia non in uno loco contenta, sed totum mundum coelesti praedicatione occupavit. Quasi aurora progreditur, quia transactis tenebris infidelitatis, lumen veritatis habere ostendit.

Pulchra ut luna. Lunam ferunt physici a sole illuminari. Et ecce Ecclesia pulchra est ut luna: quia claritate sponsi sui Christi illuminatur, et ejus gratia resplendet. Sive pulchra est ut luna in praesenti vita, ubi aliquando concessa sibi pace et tranquillitate sive securitate crescit, aliquando adversitatibus obtrita decrescit.

Electa ut sol. In alia vita, ubi perpetuo resplendebit gaudio de visione Conditoris sui. Sive electa est 1092But sol: quia illum iterum Solem imitari nititur, de quo scriptum est: Vobis timentibus nomen meum orietur sol justitiae (Malach. IV, 2). Hunc Solem imitatur vivendo in humilitate, et justitia, et pietate.

Terribilis ut castrorum acies ordinata. Terribilis aeris potestatibus, ordinata ut castrorum acies, id est charitatis unitate unita, ut nullis tentationibus penetrari valeat.

Vers. 10. Descendi ad hortum nucum. Laudata Ecclesia a Synagoga respondet: Descendi ad hortum nucum. Hortus nucum est vita praesens. Nam sicut in nuce tegmen quidem durissimum videtur, sed nucleus latet: ita in praesenti vita nostrae conscientiae clausae sunt, et non apparent, donec fracta testa corporis manifestentur. Descendit ergo ad hortum nucum Ecclesia, quando per doctores suos vitam singulorum considerat; unde sequitur:

Ut viderem poma convallium. Poma convallium sunt virtutes quae humilitate condiuntur. Descendi 1092C(inquit) ad hortum, ut viderem poma convallium, id est ut considerarem sanctos, excellentia quidem virtutum praeditos, sed humilitate depressos.

Et inspicerem si floruisset vinea. Hoc est, ut considerarem qui in studio sanctarum virtutum proficerent.

Et germinassent mala punica, hoc est, ut eos quoque perquirerem qui jam apti sunt ad imitandam passionem Christi. Nam mala punica (ut saepe dictum est) mysterium passionis Christi significant. Considerat ergo Ecclesia per praelatos suos, qui in virtutibus crescant, vel qui jam ita perfecti sunt, ut imitantes passionem Christi, pro illo quoque idonei sint sanguinem fundere, quaies erant illi quibus Apostolus dicebat: Vobis datum est non solum ut credatis in Christum, sed ut pro illo patiamini (Philip. I, 29).

527 Vers. 11. Nescivi. Vox est Synagogae, quae videns tantam Ecclesiae gratiam, inspecta veritate Evangelii, dolet quod antea non cognoverit fidem Christi; et quasi excusans se quod tam tarde conversa 1092Dsit, dicit: Nescivi, o sponsi Christi Ecclesia. Nescivi, id est non cognovi tantam gratiam, et tanta dona spiritualium virtutum tibi a sponso tuo collata.

Anima mea conturbavit me propter quadrigas Aminadab. Conturbata (inquit) sum nimia animi sollicitudine propter tam subitam Evangelii praedicationem. Sciebam enim legem et prophetas divinitus datos, et ideo cum subito vidissem praedicari Evangelium, conturbata sum propter quadrigas Aminadab. Aminadab, abnepos fuit Judae, per quem generatio Christi contexitur. Interpretatur autem Aminadab, populi mei spontaneus; ideoque significat Christum, qui spontaneus fuit populi sui: quia ipse cum esset Deus, sponte factus est homo: cum esset Conditor et Creator, sola benignitate sua factus est portio populi sui. Et est sensus: Conturbata (inquit) sum propter subitam Evangelii praedicationem, quae veluti 1093Avelocissima quadriga totum subito mundum pervolavit. Et bene hanc praedicationem non currus, sed quadrigas appellat: quia Evangelii praedicatio quatuor evangelistarum auctoritate consistit, et quatuor Evangelia quasi quatuor quadrigae sunt Novi Testamenti, cui praesidet auriga ipse Christus, temperans et disponens ipse currum Evangeliorum.

Vers. 12. Revertere, revertere Sunamitis. Vox est Ecclesiae Synagogam ad fidem invitantis: Revertere, revertere Sunamitis. Revertere, o Synagoga, ab infidelitate ad fidem, revertere ab odio ad dilectionem. Sunamitis interpretatur captiva vel despecta. Talis erat Synagoga ante adventum Christi, captiva videlicet vinculo diaboli, et despecta a Deo: quia Christum ad salutem suam missum non cognovit.

Revertere, revertere, ut intueamur te, id est, ut pulchritudinem tuae castitatis aspiciamus.

CHAPTER VI.

Verse 1. My beloved is gone down into his garden, to the bed of spice, that he may feed in the gardens, and gather lilies. We have already been taught above what the garden of Christ is, namely the holy Church, of which it was said above: A garden enclosed is my sister the bride. Christ is gone down (he says) into his own garden, to the bed, which he himself prepared and tilled, which he sowed with the seeds of the virtues, which he lit up with his own grace, which 1089Ahe defended by the wall of his own keeping from the onset of the malignant spirits. And well is he said to have gone down into his own garden; for in a garden he willed to be buried (Joan. XIX, 41), and first of all appeared to Mary Magdalen in a garden (Joan. XX, 15), and there in a manner consecrated the first-fruits of the Church. He is said to have gone down when from the higher and heavenly seats he went down into the garden which he himself had planted in a lowly place; but for this he went down, that the garden (that is, the Church) might ascend to him. Whence also in what follows he says of the garden, that is, of his Church: Who is this that ascends through the desert? To the bed (he says) of spice. By the bed is understood the soul of every faithful man, which is cultivated by heavenly discipline and diligently composed, and squared by the evenness of its sides. That he may feed (he says) in the gardens, that is, that he may be delighted by the devout labors of the saints. For he himself is fed by our good things, so far that in the poor man he himself is refreshed. And that he may gather lilies (he says), that is, that he may gather to himself out of this world the holy souls that have been brought by ripeness of virtue to perfect whiteness, 1089Band may make them rejoice with him in eternal blessedness. For the lilies are holy souls, made white by the pursuits of the virtues and by good works.

Verse 2. I to my beloved, and my beloved to me, who feeds among the lilies. I to my beloved — there is understood, I prepare a pleasing pasture; and my beloved to me will grant the grace of perfection, or the reward of eternal recompense. Or else, I to my beloved prepare a dwelling in my own self; and my beloved to me, because he himself dwells in me, and makes me dwell in him; as he himself says in the Gospel: I in you, and you in me (Joan. XIV, 20).

Verse 3. Thou art beautiful, my friend, sweet and seemly as Jerusalem. The Church was seeking her bridegroom as though he were absent; but he, that he might show himself always present, 525 and might make plain that he is always at hand to her who speaks of him and desires him, answers quickly: Thou art beautiful, my friend, sweet and seemly as Jerusalem. Thou art beautiful, because thou hast not spot nor wrinkle 1089Cof sin, but art green with every brightness of the virtues. My friend — the Church, or every faithful soul. Sweet and seemly as Jerusalem. Jerusalem, which is interpreted vision of peace, signifies that city above in which is true peace and happiness: because there the saints so rejoice over the gift, that there is nothing over which they should grieve on account of corruption. Therefore the Church, which is still on earth, or every faithful soul, is after the likeness of that Jerusalem sweet and seemly: because so far as it can it imitates that peace. There follows:

Terrible as a battle-line of the camps set in order. The Church is terrible to the malignant spirits, and she is set in order as a battle-line of the camps; when, namely, each of the faithful rightly keeps his own order and his own calling. For there are three orders of the Church: of teachers, of the continent, and of the married; which kinds were shown in those three men, Noe, Daniel and Job. For Noe, who steered the ark amid the waves, signifies the holy teachers; Daniel, unmarried and chaste, signifies the order of the continent; 1089DJob, who had a wife and sons, expresses the order of the married. Therefore the Church is set in order as a battle-line of the camps, because each order of the Church stands as in a line of battle against the enemy hosts of the malignant spirits, while the holy teachers spend their labor upon preaching and teaching. The continent, despising the things that are of the world, think only upon the things that are of God, and with a mind free from earthly occupations are at leisure for God alone. The married press on with alms and with the other good works of the active life; and so they use the world as not to forsake the things that are of God. This most well-ordered disposition of the Church therefore is terrible to the unclean spirits, as a battle-line of the camps set in order for war. Or else the Church is beautiful and seemly as Jerusalem in the virtues: but terrible as a battle-line of the camps set in order, because she does not cease to carry forth her rule and her power unto the ends of the whole world. Or she is terrible as a battle-line of the camps set in order, because she is so knit and joined together by the unity of charity, that by no force of the malignant enemy, 1090Aby no plague of discord can she be pierced; for nothing so terrifies the malignant spirits as charity does.

Verse 4. Turn away thy eyes from me, for they have made me fly away. As if he were to say: I indeed gave thee dove-like eyes, by which thou mightest gaze upon me and pierce most keenly into the secrets of the Scriptures; but beware lest thou wish to strain them now to seeing me: because while thou livest in this present life, thou canst not; For man shall not see me, and live (Exod. XXXIII, 20). But when, loosed from the bonds of the flesh, thou hast come to me, then shalt thou see me with open vision, and there shall be fulfilled what is promised in the Gospel: But he that loveth me shall be loved by my Father, and I will love him, and will manifest myself to him (Joan. XIV, 21). But as for what is said, for they have made me fly away, it is not to be thought that God forsakes those who seek him, and withdraws from them, since he himself commands: Seek, and you shall find (Luc. XI, 9); but it is rather so to be taken, that the more anyone has wished to search out the divinity of the majesty of God, the 1090Bmore does he understand how unsearchable and incomprehensible it is. And this is what the Psalmist says: Man shall come to a deep heart, and God shall be exalted (Psal. LXIII, 7). As if he were to say: Although a man lift up his heart to seeing God, God shall be exalted, because how great he is cannot be grasped.

Thy hair is as a flock of goats which have appeared from Galaad. These little verses have been expounded more than once; but let it not irk us to expound again what it did not irk the Holy Spirit to repeat. For he set down these little verses twice for this reason, that we might hold as most certain and settled what we heard confirmed by repetition. The hairs of the holy Church are the manifold varieties of subtle thoughts. And they are well compared to a flock of goats; for the goat is a figure of the sinner; whence also it is commanded in the law that whoever has sinned through ignorance and has come to know his guilt should offer for his offense a she-goat for a holocaust (Lev. IV, 28). The hairs therefore of the Church are compared to a flock of goats, because the saints, who are members of the Church, take pains to blot out all the guilt of their thought by worthy 1090Cpenance and by the tears of just compunction. And well are they said to have appeared from the mountain of Galaad. Galaad is interpreted a heap of witness; and therefore by Galaad is signified the mind of the saints, who are a heap of witness, since it attests by the most evident tokens that it disregards all things that are of the world, and desires God alone. By Galaad also can our Lord and Redeemer himself be understood. For Galaad is the name both of a mountain and of a city. From the mountain therefore of Galaad the flock of goats appeared, because the Church is founded upon Christ, as he himself says: A city seated upon a mountain cannot be hid (Matth. V, 15). He himself is the heap of witness: because the multitude of the prophets and of the patriarchs bear him witness by their deeds and their writings.

Verse 5. Thy teeth are as flocks of sheep, which have come up from the washing, all with twin young, and there is not a barren one among them. By the teeth of the Church is understood the firm steadfastness of her words. And these are well compared to a flock of sheep that have come up from the washing, because the words of the Church sound nothing but innocence and meekness, 1090Dand keep in themselves nothing foul, nothing base or unclean; but they are washed in the washing of sincerity and purity, hearing from the Apostle: Let not evil speech proceed out of your mouth (Ephes. IV, 29). And well are they said to be all with twin young, because the speech of the saints sounds nothing else nor utters anything, save what belongs to the love of God and of the neighbor. For the saints ought to speak those things only which pertain to the everlasting soundness of bodies and of souls.

Verse 6. As is the rind of the pomegranate, so are thy cheeks, apart from thy hidden things. We said above that in the cheeks the chaste modesty of the saints is figured; in the rind of the pomegranate, the mystery of the passion of Christ, as we hear from Paul the apostle: Be not ashamed of the cross of Christ, nor of me his prisoner (II Tim. I, 8) [Vulg. testimonium Domini nostri, "the testimony of our Lord," for "the cross of Christ"]. And these things indeed are great, which are in the open: but greatest are those which are within, that is, those which are kept in store for the saints in time to come.

1091AVerse 7. There are sixty queens, and eighty concubines, and of young maidens there is no number. The queens are the teachers of the holy Church, who by the merit of faith and of knowledge draw near to the couch of the eternal King, and bear spiritual sons to God. The number sixty is completed out of ten and six. For ten multiplied by six 526 rises into sixty. And by ten are signified the divine precepts, on account of the Decalogue of the law; but six is put for perfection, because in six days God finished his works. The queens therefore are said to be sixty, because they signify those, as we have said, who are strong in perfection of knowledge and of work, and who preach for the love alone of their Creator and with a view to the recompense above. This number also, that is, sixty, is made up out of twelve taken five times; for five twelves, or twelve fives, make sixty. The queens therefore are said to be sixty, because they signify those who, being set in the Church, have known how chastely 1091Bto order the five senses of their body according to the institution of the twelve apostles. The concubines signify those who preach Christ not sincerely, but for the sake of temporal gain, or for the sake of popular praise. For these also, like the queens, come to the couch of the King through knowledge, and beget spiritual sons; but they stand estranged from the crown of the eternal kingdom. And well are they comprised under the number eighty; for this number is made up out of ten and eight. But eighty is sometimes taken in a bad sense on account of the number four, by the multiplying of which it consists. For four signifies all things temporal and present, on account of the four regions of the world or the four seasons of the year. Rightly therefore are these concubines said to be eighty: because (as has been said) they preach for the sake of temporal and visible things alone, and care less for things spiritual. Of such the Apostle said: There are some who announce Christ not sincerely, etc. (Philip. I, 17, 18); until he says: in this I rejoice, yea and I will rejoice. Of young maidens there is no number. The young maidens are souls already regenerate in Christ, 1091Cwho, having put off the oldness of the first man, have been renewed in Christ, and yet are not yet marriageable, not yet fit for the couch of the King; because they have not yet come to the perfection of preaching Christ. And of these there is no number, because infinite is the number of souls believing in Christ, nor can it be grasped by any man. For the rest, all the elect are numbered by God; for with him, by whom even the number of the stars is comprehended (Psal. CXLVI, 4), the number of the elect cannot be unknown.

Verse 8. One is my dove, my perfect one. That is, one is the catholic Church spread throughout the whole world, which, consisting of queens and of young maidens, or of young men, has also concubines, that is, certain persons who are called Christians or teachers in name only. One therefore is the Church, because she admits no rending or schisms; but as there is one God, one faith, one baptism (Ephes. IV, 5), so there is one general Church, which is rightly also called a dove: because by the dowry of the Holy Spirit, who appeared in the form of a dove, 1091Dshe was betrothed and sanctified to Christ.

She is one to her mother, the chosen one of her that bore her. Our mother is that heavenly Jerusalem, of which the Apostle says: But that Jerusalem which is above is free, which is our mother. This Church therefore is one to her mother, because after the example of that Church which already enjoys Christ, she herself is instructed and formed in this pilgrimage, and strives to come to that blessedness in which she reigns together with Christ.

The daughters of Sion saw her, and the queens proclaimed her most blessed, and the concubines praised her. Those whom above he called young maidens, here he calls daughters. But the queens and concubines let us understand in this place as above. The daughters therefore, the queens and the concubines proclaim and praise the Church, because the whole body of the faithful marvels at the catholic Church. For that same Church, which consists of many persons of the faithful, makes up the catholic, that is, the universal Church.

1092AVerse 9. Who is this that comes forth as the morning rising? It is the voice of the Synagogue. For at the end of the world, when the Jewish people has been converted to Christ at the preaching of Elias and Enoch, she too will marvel at the Church, saying: Who is this? that is, how great and of what sort is the Church, that comes forth? that is, that goes forward from virtue to virtue. For the Church is said not to stand still, but to come forth: because she was not content with one place, but has taken possession of the whole world by her heavenly preaching. She comes forth as the morning, because, the darkness of unbelief being past, she has shown that she has the light of truth.

Beautiful as the moon. The natural philosophers report that the moon is lit up by the sun. And behold, the Church is beautiful as the moon: because she is lit up by the brightness of her bridegroom Christ, and shines again with his grace. Or she is beautiful as the moon in this present life, where sometimes, peace and tranquillity or security being granted her, she waxes, and sometimes, worn down by adversities, she wanes.

Chosen as the sun. In the other life, where she will shine again with perpetual joy from the vision of her Creator. Or she is chosen 1092Bas the sun: because she strives to imitate that other Sun of whom it is written: To you that fear my name the sun of justice shall arise (Malach. IV, 2). This Sun she imitates by living in humility, and justice, and godliness.

Terrible as a battle-line of the camps set in order. Terrible to the powers of the air, set in order as a battle-line of the camps, that is, made one by the unity of charity, so that she can be pierced by no temptations.

Verse 10. I went down into the garden of nuts. The Church, being praised by the Synagogue, answers: I went down into the garden of nuts. The garden of nuts is this present life. For as in a nut the shell indeed is seen to be very hard, but the kernel lies hidden: so in this present life our consciences are shut up, and do not appear, until, the shell of the body being broken, they are made manifest. The Church therefore goes down into the garden of nuts when through her teachers she considers the life of each; whence there follows:

That I might see the fruits of the valleys. The fruits of the valleys are the virtues which are seasoned with humility. I went down 1092C(she says) into the garden, that I might see the fruits of the valleys, that is, that I might consider the saints, endowed indeed with excellence of virtues, but pressed down by humility.

And to look whether the vineyard had flourished. That is, that I might consider who were making progress in the pursuit of holy virtues.

And whether the pomegranates had budded, that is, that I might search out those also who are now fit to imitate the passion of Christ. For the pomegranates (as has often been said) signify the mystery of the passion of Christ. The Church therefore considers, through her prelates, who are growing in the virtues, or who are now so perfect that, imitating the passion of Christ, they are fit also to shed their blood for him; such as quaies were they to whom the Apostle said: To you it is given not only to believe in Christ, but to suffer for him (Philip. I, 29).

527 Verse 11. I knew not. It is the voice of the Synagogue, who, seeing so great a grace of the Church, and having looked into the truth of the Gospel, grieves that she did not before know the faith of Christ; and as though excusing herself for having been converted so late, 1092Dshe says: I knew not, O Church, bride of Christ. I knew not, that is, I did not recognize so great a grace, and such great gifts of spiritual virtues bestowed upon thee by thy bridegroom.

My soul troubled me because of the chariots of Aminadab. I was troubled (she says) by an exceeding anxiety of mind because of so sudden a preaching of the Gospel. For I knew that the law and the prophets had been given from God, and therefore, when on a sudden I saw the Gospel preached, I was troubled because of the chariots of Aminadab. Aminadab was the great-great-grandson of Judah, through whom the generation of Christ is woven together. Now Aminadab is interpreted the willing one of my people; and therefore he signifies Christ, who was the willing one of his people: because he himself, being God, of his own will was made man; being Founder and Creator, of his own kindness alone he was made a portion of his own people. And the sense is: I was troubled (she says) because of the sudden preaching of the Gospel, which as it were 1093Awith a most swift chariot suddenly flew over the whole world. And well does she call this preaching not a car, but chariots: because the preaching of the Gospel stands upon the authority of four evangelists, and the four Gospels are as it were the four chariots of the New Testament, over which Christ himself presides as charioteer, himself guiding and ordering the car of the Gospels.

Verse 12. Return, return, O Sunamite. It is the voice of the Church calling the Synagogue to the faith: Return, return, O Sunamite. Return, O Synagogue, from unbelief to faith, return from hatred to love. Sunamite is interpreted captive or despised. Such was the Synagogue before the coming of Christ, captive namely in the bond of the devil, and despised by God: because she did not recognize Christ, who was sent for her salvation.

Return, return, that we may behold thee, that is, that we may look upon the beauty of thy chastity.

CAPUT VII.

1093B

Vers. 1. Quid videbis in Sunamite, nisi choros castrorum? Vox est sponsi, qui audiens invitari Synagogam a sponsa sua Ecclesia ad fidem, dicit: Quid videbis in Sunamite, nisi choros castrorum? ac si diceret: Tu quidem, o sponsa, doles de perditione Synagogae, et quod tarde ad fidem veniat; sed adest tempus quo convertatur, et tunc nihil videbis in ea nisi choros castrorum, id est, nisi charitatis concordiam et stabile robur fidei, quo pro defensione meae fidei pugnabit. Nam chori, canentium sunt: castra vero, militum pugnantium. In Sunamite ergo chori castrorum videbuntur, quia conversa Synagoga et concorditer Christum praedicabit cum Ecclesia, et pro fide Christi usque ad mortem pugnabit.

Quam pulchri sunt gressus tui! Ab hoc loco incipit sponsus exponere laudes sponsae suae. Et notandum quod a gressibus incipit ejus pulchritudinem texere, et in ore laudes ejus finit, sicut superius ab oculis 1093Ccoeperat. Hoc autem fecit vel ad pulchritudinem carminis variandam, vel etiam quia sic mysteriis aptissimum est. Quam pulchri (inquit) sunt gressus tui! Vidit Ecclesiam non otiose in uno loco stantem, sed veloci praedicatione totum mundum peragrantem, ideoque a gressibus coepit eam laudare.: Gressus enim Ecclesiae sunt velox praedicatio apostolorum, per quos in omnem terram Evangelium insonuit.

In calceamentis, id est in patrum praecedentium exemplis. Calceamenta enim ex mortuis fiunt animalibus. Et calceamenta Ecclesiae sunt patrum praecedentium exempla, quibus gressus habet munitos; ut securius eum imitando, et sine offensione gradiatur.

Filia principis. Alia translatio, filia Aminadab dicit. Aminadab (ut superius dictum est) populi mei spontaneus dicitur, qui recte figuram Christi tenet. Nam Ecclesia et filia est, et sponsa: filia, quia ejus sanguine redempta, ejus baptismate regenerata; 1093Dsponsa, quia dote Spiritus sancti illi est conjuncta.

Juncturae femorum tuorum sicut monilia quae fabricata sunt manu artificis. In feminibus, vel in femoribus solet sacra Scriptura generationis propaginem designare. Unde de Jacob legimus: Omnes animae quae egressae sunt de femore Jacob (Exod. I, 5). Femina ergo Ecclesiae sunt spiritualis generatio credentis populi per verbum praedicationis et lavacrum regenerationis. Juncturae femorum est conjunctio duorum populorum in una fide, Judaici et gentilis. Et bene haec junctura monilibus comparatur: quia fides utriusque populi maxime in operibus manifestatur; nam fides sine operibus mortua est (Jac. II, 26). Fabricata autem sunt manu artificis, hoc est, Dei omnipotentis, cujus munere virtus boni operis credentibus tribuitur. Hic est ille artifex de quo Paulus dicebat, quando memoriam Abrahae patriarchae faciebat: 1094AExspectabat enim manentem civitatem, cujus conditor et artifex Deus est (Hebr. XI, 10).

Vers. 2. Umbilicus tuus crater tornatilis, nunquam indigens poculis. Umbilicus, fragilissima est pars corporis, et ideo per umbilicum fragilitatem nostrae mortalitatis debemus accipere. Crater vero est calix major ansas hinc inde habens. Umbilicus ergo Ecclesiae, crater est tornatilis: quia quanto magis doctores Ecclesiae suae fragilitatis meminerint, tanto magis verbo praedicationis insistunt; ut eleemosynam verbi Dei praebentes, ipsi misericordiam a Deo consequantur. Et bene crater tornatilis dicitur: torno enim facilius operamur; per quod significatur facilitas erogandi verbum, vel eleemosynam dandi. Non enim morose, nec haesitando eleemosyna danda est; juxta quod Salomon dicit: Ne dixeris amico tuo: Vade et revertere, cras dabo tibi, cum statim possis dare (Prov. III, 26). Quod etiam torno fit, non eget exterius adhibita regula vel linea: sed inerrate ex seipso perficitur. Umbilicus ergo Ecclesiae crater est 1094Btornatilis, quia quicunque vel praedicat, vel eleemosynam dat, pro sola dilectione et supernae me cedis exspectatione hoc facere debet. Nam quicunque vel praedicat, vel proximis miseretur pro remuneratione temporali vel laude, crater est, sed non tornatilis. Et hic crater nunquam indiget poculis, quia praedicatoribus nunquam deest praedicatio verbi Dei: quia ipse quem praedicant, pocula scientiae, et virtutem constantiae illis subministrat; unde et Psalmista: Dominus dabit verbum evangelizantibus virtute multa (Psal. LXVII, 12).

528 Venter tuus sicut acervus tritici, vallatus liliis. Eadem fragilitas quae per umbilicum designata est, ostenditur hoc loco per ventrem, quia venter nulla ossium firmitate praemunitur. Bene ergo venter Ecclesiae acervo tritici comparatur, quia sancti quique quanto magis memores sunt suae fragilitatis, eo studiosius ad proferendos bonorum operum fructus insistunt. Et recte non copiam tritici, sed acervum 1094Ctritici dicit, ut per hoc significet incrementa virtutum, quae veluti acervus, ab inferioribus ad superiora crescendo conscendunt. Nam sicut acervus in inferioribus lata amplitudine spatiatur, superius vero quadam brevitate contrahitur et acuminatur: ita in Ecclesia multi sunt qui indulgentius vivunt, pauci vero qui summae perfectioni insistunt. Plures enim inveniuntur qui de bonis suis eleemosynam faciunt, quam qui sua pro Deo relinquunt. Hic acervus liliis vallatus dicitur, quia sancti bona quae faciunt, pro desiderio supernae claritatis et candoris agunt. Nam per lilia coelestis illa claritas designatur. Potest etiam per ventrem Ecclesiae divinus fons baptismatis accipi, quo fideles in novam creaturam regenerantur. Hic ergo venter acervo tritici vallato liliis comparatur, quia regenerati fonte baptismatis docentur bonis insudare operibus, et ad intuendam supernae claritatis gloriam semper inhiare.

Vers. 3. Duo ubera tua sicut duo hinnuli gemelli 1094Dcapreae. Ubera Ecclesiae (sicut jam superius dictum est) sanctos doctores significant, qui parvulos quosque (hoc est, nuper in Christo regeneratos) lacte apertioris et facilioris doctrinae instruunt. Bene autem duo ubera dicuntur, quia de duobus populis, Judaico videlicet et gentili fideles congregantur, et lacte spiritualis doctrinae aluntur. Haec duo ubera, sicut duo hinnuli capreae gemelli esse dicuntur. Duo hinnuli sunt duo Testamenta, quibus eorumdem doctorum omnis praedicatio subsistit, quorum Testamentorum unus est auctor et promulgator Christus, juxta quod Salomon dicit: Verba sapientium quasi stimuli, et quasi clari in altum defixi, quae data sunt a pastore uno (Eccli. XII, 11), Christo videlicet. Caprea enim recte Christum significat, quia et intuitu et velocitate caeteris animantibus praeeminet, et mundum est animal, ungulam dividens et ruminans. Tales enim sunt membra Christi, doctores videlicet, intuitu 1095Ascientiae insignes, et ea velocitate terrena deserentes, atque ad coelestia festinantes. Dividens ungulam, id est habens boni et mali discretionem; et ruminans, hoc est sedula memoria praecepta Dei meditans.

Vers. 4. Collum tuum sicut turris eburnea. Doctores sancti, qui designantur per ubera, ipsi designantur et per collum. Nam et per arterias colli vox egreditur, et colli ministerio caeteris membris vitalia alimenta ministrantur: sic et sancti doctores et verba vitae populis annuntiant, et alimento doctrinae coelestis eos nutriunt. Collum ergo Ecclesiae turri eburneae comparatur: quia sancti doctores eidem Ecclesiae et pulchritudinem et robur praestant; pulchritudinem quidem, quae per candorem eboris designatur, caste et sincere vivendo; robur vero praestant, quod significatur per turrim, quando eamdem Ecclesiam contra tentationes diaboli vel impetus haereticorum muniunt.

Oculi tui sicut piscinae in Hesebon, quae sunt in porta 1095Bfiliae multitudinis. Id est ipsi doctores, qui per collum designati sunt, designantur et per oculos. Ipsi enim quasi oculi universo corpori Ecclesiae provident, et iter quod ingredi debeant ostendunt. Hi oculi Ecclesiae comparantur piscinis aedificatis in porta civitatis Hesebon, quia sancti doctores populos in Christum credentes, vitali lavacro abluunt, et salutaris doctrinae potu reficiunt. Filia autem multitudinis vocatur Hesebon juxta litteram, ob multitudinem confluentium inibi populorum. Sic et sancta Ecclesia recte dicitur filia multitudinis, quia numerositatem quotidie colligit gentium. Hesebon autem interpretatur cingulum moeroris. Oculi ergo Ecclesiae piscinis Hesebon comparantur, quia sancti doctores, neglectis caducis saeculi gaudiis, cingulo moeroris se constringunt: quia caste et sobrie vivunt in hoc saeculo (Tit. II, 12), et pro suis erratibus deflent; quatenus in praesenti saeculo lugentes, ad vera aeternaque possint pervenire gaudia. In porta autem 1095DHesebon hae piscinae esse dicuntur: quia nullus Ecclesiam ingredi valet, nisi prius aqua baptismatis abluatur, et nisi fonte salutaris doctrinae potetur. Hoc etiam significabat labrum aeneum quod Salomon in porticu templi posuerat, ut sacerdotes ingressuri templum, et hostias immolaturi, ibi corpora abluerent (III Reg. VII, 23).

Nasus tuus sicut turris Libani, quae respicit contra Damascum. Quia naso fetores et bonos odores discernimus, recte per nasum Ecclesiae iidem sancti doctores intelliguntur. Ipsi enim sagaciter discernere noverunt fragrantem catholicae fidei doctrinam, et lethiferum haeretici erroris fetorem. Nam et in divinis charismatibus, quae per Spiritum sanctum distribuuntur, dicitur: Alii datur per Spiritum discretio spirituum (I Cor. XII, 10). Nasus ergo Ecclesiae turri Libani comparatur, quia sancti doctores et summum locum in Ecclesia tenent, ac veluti in Libano monte consistunt, et defendunt Ecclesiam a malignorum spirituum incursibus. Haec turris contra 1095DDamascum esse dicitur. Damascus metropolis civitas est Syriae, quae quondam contra filios Israel, ut pote fortissimos et crudelissimos reges habens, dimicabat. Interpretatur autem Damascus, sanguinis potus, vel sanguinis oculus. Ferunt ibi Abel interfectum, ideoque significat potestates hujus saeculi, quae sanguinis potum sitiunt: quia voluptatibus et illecebris carnis et sanguinis delectantur. Significat etiam aeras potestates, quae animarum nostrarum cruorem sitiunt. Contra Damascum ergo est haec turris: quia haec Ecclesia semper diabolo et membris illius, adjutorio Christi munita, resistit.

Vers. 5. Caput tuum ut Carmelus. Caput ergo Ecclesiae, principalitas mentis fidelium intelligitur; nam sicut capite membra reguntur caetera; ita mente cogitationes disponuntur. Bene autem caput Ecclesiae Carmelo comparatur. Carmelus enim, qui et Carmel dicitur, interpretatur cognitio vel scientia circumcisionis. 1096AErgo caput Ecclesiae Carmelo assimilatur: quia novit circumcisionem aliquando corporaliter celebratam, nunc se spiritualiter observare debere, juxta quod propheta dicit: Circumcidimini Domino in cordibus vestris (Jer. IV, 2). Hanc sententiam circumcisionis non habuerunt Judaei, ideoque reprehenduntur a beato Stephano protomartyre 529 dicente: Dura cervice et incircumcisi cordibus et auribus, semper Spiritui sancto restitistis (Act. VII, 51). In libro quoque Regum legitur (III Reg. XVIII, 45) quod Elias in montem Carmeli ascenderit, et ibi orando aridam terram coelestibus pluviis irrigaverit post longam trium annorum et semis siccitatem. Quae historia pulchram redolet allegoriam; nam Elias, qui interpretatur Deus meus Dominus, Christum significat, qui cum sit Deus et Dominus omnium et totius creaturae, specialiter suus, id est proprius est Deus et Dominus Ecclesiae. Hic in Carmelum ascendit, quando per gratiam suam mentes fidelium suorum conscendit, et per eos arenti mundo gratiarum suarum pluvias 1096Btribuit. Recte ergo (ut dictum est) caput Ecclesiae Carmelo monti comparatur: quia mens sanctorum quadam conversatione sublimis est, et Christo in se ascensum praebet per profectum virtutum.

Et comae capitis tui ut purpura regis vincta canalibus. Si per caput Ecclesiae mens accipitur, per comas capitis cogitationes mentis debemus accipere, quae assidue de illa prodeunt. Purpureus vero color, qui sanguinis habet speciem, mysterium dominicae passionis significat. Juxta litteram quod dicit, purpura regis vincta canalibus, significat lanam jam purpureo colore confectam, et necdum in fila deductam, sed adhuc in canalibus constitutam, quas Graece βάτια vocant, in quibus sanguis conchyliorum solet defluere, et lanam in purpureum colorem vertere. Mystice canales isti humilitatem sanctorum significant, in quibus aeterni Regis purpura tingitur, cum sancti quique humilitatem sui Redemptoris imitando, passionum illius conformes fieri satagunt; quatenus in 1096Cpurpurae dignitatem vertantur, id est laborantes pro Christo, mereantur coronari et regnare cum Christo. Vincta autem haec purpura esse dicitur, quia sanctorum praecordia fixa sunt et stabilita in timore et amore Redemptoris sui, nec unquam a charitate ejus separari possunt.

Vers. 6. Quam pulchra es, et quam decora, charissima, in deliciis! Quam pulchra es, scilicet in fide, et decora in operatione, charissima, in deliciis. Supra dictum est de eadem Ecclesia: Pulchra ut luna, electa ut sol, terribilis ut castrorum acies ordinata; hic vero dicitur, pulchra et decora, charissima, in deliciis. Quae quasi contraria esse videntur; nam ordinatam esse velut castrorum aciem, et esse in deliciis, repugnat, quia deliciae fortitudinem militum solent enervare. Sed sancta Ecclesia utrumque agit; ordinata est velut castrorum acies, quia infidelibus haereticis resistit, et malignos spiritus sua sanctitate et perfectione terret: in deliciis etiam est, quia suavitatem supernae dulcedinis assidue palato mentis degustat, et inter 1096Dpressuras hujus saeculi illas adipisci desiderat, et continue illis satiari. Unde merito illa quae talis est, sponso suo Christo charissima esse dicitur.

Vers. 7. Statura tua assimilata est pulmae. Statura Ecclesiae est perfecta ejus rectitudo; neque enim ad vitia incurvatur, sed recta et immobilis persistit. Unde Psalmista ex persona peccantium dicebat: Incurvatus sum et humiliatus usquequaque (Psal. CXVIII, 107). Et propheta sub specie Jerusalem, peccanti animae de malignis spiritibus dicit: Quia humiliaverunt te, et dixerunt animae tuae: Incurvare ut transeamus (Isa. LI, 23).
