Christ therefore admonishes her by saying this, that she may cease from envy and may rejoice together at the salvation of the Church.

The lamps thereof are lamps of fire and of flames. That is, the lamps of love are lamps of fire and of flames. The lamps of love are the hearts of the saints, in which, as in vessels, love dwells. These lamps, therefore, are of fire and of flames: lamps indeed of fire, because they burn in the heart 1103Aby love; but lamps of flames, because outwardly they shine by work.

Verse 7. Many waters cannot quench charity, neither shall the floods overwhelm it. The waters and the floods are the violent threats of persecutors, or also their blandishments, by which they strive to separate the saints from the charity of God. Of which the Lord says in the Gospel, concerning the man who built his house upon a rock: The floods came, the winds blew, and they beat upon that house, and it fell not; for it was built upon a rock (Matth. VII, 25). Many waters, therefore, cannot quench charity, because no tribulation, no distress will be able to separate the saints from the charity of God.

If a man should give all the substance of his house for love, he shall despise it as nothing. This little verse needs no exposition; for the substances of no world, no riches, are anything in comparison of love. We read indeed that the saints left their own goods for the love of Christ, and yet they seemed to themselves to leave nothing in 1103Bcomparison of the charity of Christ, whom they loved. Whence the Apostle, when he was leaving for Christ not only his earthly substance but also the traditions of his fathers, said: All these things I despise and count as dung, that I may gain Christ (Philip. III, 8), that is, that I may acquire his love.

Verse 8. Our sister is little, and she hath no breasts. What shall we do to our sister in the day when she is to be spoken to? The voice of the beloved addressing the Synagogue on behalf of the Church of the nations. Our sister, he says — that is, the Church of the nations — is little, and hath no breasts; and, the name being shared with the Synagogue by a great condescension, he says, Our sister; for because he took flesh from the Jews, therefore he as it were tempers himself to the Synagogue and makes himself her equal. Our sister is little, he says. As the age of any man is described, so also the age of the Church is described. For the Church as yet gathered from the nations was little under the very times of the Lord and of the apostles: because by the fewness of believers she was little; 1103Cand she had not yet breasts, that is, she was by no means able by preaching to beget spiritual sons for God. He says therefore, as though seeking the counsel of the Synagogue: What shall we do to our sister in the day when she is to be spoken to? The speaking to the Church is when God admonishes the soul of every faithful person, either inwardly by hidden inspiration or outwardly by open preaching. And the sense is: when through my apostles I shall speak to the Church gathered from the nations, what shall we do? how shall we entrust to her the heavenly precepts? Whether, namely, we shall entrust to her the least precepts, or, that she may be made greater, shall we deliver to her the more perfect mysteries of the heavenly secrets? For God almighty tempers the word of preaching to the measure and capacity of the human mind, considering the strength and capacity of the hearer. But the Synagogue being silent, the bridegroom himself answered for her.

Verse 9. If she be a wall, let us build upon it bulwarks of silver; if she be a door, let us join it together with boards of cedar. If, he says, our sister be a wall, 1103Dlet us build upon it bulwarks of silver. As if he should say: When my apostles shall have begun to preach to the Church gathered from the nations, if the hearers shall be such as can deservedly be called a wall for their perfection — when, namely, being either endowed with natural talent or excelling in philosophical learning, they can as it were defend and fortify others in the stead of a wall — let us build upon such a wall bulwarks of silver, that is, let us add to them the eloquences of the divine Scriptures. For, as the Psalmist says: The words of the Lord are chaste words, silver tried by the fire (Psal. XI, 7). But if the hearers shall be such as can by no means resist enemies, nor beat back the weapons that come against them, but shall be as it were a door, so that, announcing simple things, they can bring some into the Church and unto the faith — such as are the priests of lesser rank, who by baptizing in the Church and by initiating men into the divine mysteries bring men into the Church — let us join together that door with boards of cedar, that is, let us set before them the examples of the Fathers who went before, 1104Awho like cedars — woods, namely, that cannot rot — have unfading works, and like boards are spread out in the breadth of knowledge and of charity. For the breadth of the boards signifies the breadth of the heart (in which there ought to be the breadth of charity or of knowledge), the Psalmist saying: I have run the way of thy commandments, when thou didst enlarge my heart (Psal. CXVIII, 32). With these boards of cedar, therefore (that is, with the examples of the saints), let us join together that door; so that, namely, by imitating their examples they may open to others, as a door, the gate of entering the Church.

Verse 10. I am a wall, and my breasts are as a tower. The voice of the Church. Who, when she had heard her beloved taking counsel for her salvation, herself answered gladly: I am a wall, and my breasts are as a tower. I, she says, am a wall: because I am founded upon the firm rock 534 (Matth. XVI, 18), and made solid with the glue of divine charity. Or I am a wall, because I am built of living and chosen stones (that is, of the saints). And my breasts are as a tower, because I contain within me such as can, 1104Bin the stead of breasts, nourish others with spiritual doctrine, and, in the stead of a tower, can defend and fortify others; and who, as towers upon a lofty wall, so they themselves excel among the rest of my members by the merit of their doctrine and of their manner of life. But this I have not by my own merits, nor by the freedom of my will, but by the gift and grace of my bridegroom.

From the time that I became before him as one finding peace. That is, from the time that he poured out his blood for me and, propitiating God the Father toward me, dissolved the enmities that were between God and man, and made the things of heaven peaceable for me. From the time, therefore, that I found this peace, I have deserved to be a wall and to have breasts in the stead of towers.

Verse 11. The peaceable one had a vineyard, that is, my beloved, who is the true peaceable one: because through him all things are made peaceable that are in the heavens and on the earth. Whence also by the prophet he is called Father of the world to come, Prince of peace (Isai. IX, 6): in the type of which true peaceable one there went before Salomon, that is, peaceable, the author of this song. To him, therefore, 1104Cthere was a vineyard, that is, the holy Church.

In that which hath peoples. This he says, that he might show that he sings of the Church of the nations, not of the Synagogue. For the Synagogue was of one nation only, but the Church has peoples: because she holds and receives all the nations within her most capacious bosom.

He delivered it to keepers. The keepers of this vineyard (that is, of the holy Church) are the prophets, the apostles, and the successors of the apostles. Or also the keepers of this world are the angelic powers, who keep the Church from the assaults of temptations and of demons.

A man bringeth for the fruit thereof a thousand pieces of silver. He means strong and perfect men, for vir, a man, is so named from virtus, strength. The fruit of the Church is the reward of the heavenly life, for which the holy Church ceases not to labour in the present life. And the thousand pieces of silver which the man bringeth for the fruit signify all things that can be possessed in this world. For the number of a thousand is set for the highest perfection and fulness. The man, therefore, bringeth a thousand pieces of silver for the fruit of this vineyard: because the saints leave all 1104Dtheir own goods and pay them out to the poor, that they may obtain the fruit of this vineyard (that is, the reward above). Such also is that which the Lord says in the parable of the Gospel: The kingdom of heaven is like unto a treasure hidden in a field, which when a man hath found, he hideth it, and for joy thereof goeth and selleth all that he hath, and buyeth that field (Matth. XIII, 44).

Verse 12. My vineyard is before me. The voice of the bridegroom. Thou sayest, he says, that I delivered my vineyard to keepers: yet the vineyard is always before me; because, although I have committed her to my keepers, I nevertheless always have care of her, nor do I cease ever to be solicitous for her, but I am with her even unto the consummation of the world.

A thousand are thy peaceable ones, and two hundred to them that keep the fruits thereof. A thousand — understand, pieces of silver, which thou gavest for the fruit of the vineyard — are thy peaceable ones; as if he should say: O faithful soul, be not doubtful concerning the eternal reward, for which thou hast given all that is thine: because the thousand pieces of silver are thy peaceable 1105Aones, that is, that reward awaits thee assured, and thou shalt obtain true fruit therefrom. And two hundred — understand, pieces of silver — are peaceable to them that keep the fruits thereof. As by the number of a thousand the fulness of perfection, so also by the number of a hundred the highest perfection is designated: because the hundred is carried over from the left hand to the right. The two hundred pieces of silver, therefore, which is the hundred doubled, signify those who are perfect in doctrine and in holy work, who by living justly and piously keep themselves, and also by preaching convert others. To such as these, therefore, the two hundred pieces of silver are peaceable: because a doubled reward awaits them in the heavens; of whom it is said through Daniel: And they that instruct many unto justice shall shine as stars unto perpetual eternities (Dan. XII, 3). For these the Apostle bids be doubly honoured, saying: Let the priests that are among you be accounted worthy of double honour, especially they that rule over you and labour in the word (I Tim. V, 17).

Verse 13. Thou that dwellest in the gardens, the friends hearken: make me hear thy voice. The voice of the beloved addressing the bride, that 1105Bis, the Church: O Church, thou that dwellest in the gardens, that is, thou that appliest thyself to the cultivating of the shoots of the virtues. For the garden of the bridegroom is the Church herself, which (as was said above) he promises to visit often. The Church, therefore, dwells in the gardens: because she continually nourishes the nurseries of the virtues; nor does she set up her hut there in passing, as one about to withdraw after a little, but she dwells there in a fixed station. Make me, he says, hear thy voice, that is, preach the Gospel to those to whom thou art able; announce the precepts of my law, and together with them the promise of the heavenly rewards. For this is pleasant and delightful to me, that I hear thy voice, that is, that I hear thee announcing the words of eternal salvation. But the friends hearken to thee, that is, the angelic spirits delight to hear thy voice, whom I made thy friends by the shedding of my blood, and whom I appointed as helpers and protectors against the malignant spirits. Or the friends, that is, the spirits of the just, hearken, whom I have taken up out of thy company to myself into the heavenly places. For they rejoice together over thy preaching and over the salvation of the brethren. Straightway 1105Cthe bride herself answers her beloved.

Verse 14. Flee away, my beloved, and be thou like to the roe and to the young hart upon the mountains of spices. A wondrous answer. She is invited to preach, and that the bridegroom may hear her voice; but she exhorts her bridegroom 1106Ato flee. But great is the sequence of the mystery. For because above the nativity of Christ had been foretold, when she said: Who shall give thee to me for my brother, sucking the breasts: for his passion had been foretold, where it was said: Under the apple tree I raised thee up; now she consequently exhorts the bridegroom to flee, that is, most swiftly to return by the ascension to the Father. And the sense is: thou biddest me preach, thou willest to hear my voice — flee away, my beloved, that is, the mystery of thy incarnation and of thy passion being fulfilled, return to heaven; that I may begin to think of thee no longer as man, but as God above all things. For then thou wilt hear my voice, because then I shall preach thee more freely to the whole world. And this is what the Lord says in the Gospel: I tell you the truth, it is expedient for you that I go (Joan. XVI, 7). For it was expedient for the apostles and for the whole Church that Christ should return and should absent his bodily presence: because while he was present in the body they could not love him spiritually, nor could they think of him whom they beheld as man as God invisible and everywhere present. 1106B535 But after he himself ascended the heavens, the Church began to love him spiritually, and so now to think of him and to preach him as God. And this is what the Apostle says: And if we have known Christ according to the flesh, yet now we know him no more (II Cor. V, 16). Therefore, my beloved, if thou wilt that I so preach thee, flee away, that is, withdraw to the heavenly places; and thou who wast comprehensible through thy humanity, be made incomprehensible through thy divinity. Yet nevertheless forsake me not in the present without thy visitation, but deign to seek me out with thy frequent visitation, and to offer thyself to me more often to be seen. And this is what she says: Be thou like to the roe and to the young hart upon the mountains of spices. As, she says, roes and the hinauli of harts, untamed animals, flee from human company, and yet are the more often seen upon the mountains: so thou too after thy ascension, although thou be invisible, yet deign to appear to me more often by the gift of thy grace, and this upon the mountains of spices. The mountains of spices are the saints of God, nearest to heaven by the excellence of their holy manner of life, and 1106Cfragrant far and wide with the odour and report of their virtues; hence the Apostle says of himself and of those like him: Our conversation is in heaven (Philip. III, 20); and elsewhere: We are the good odour of Christ unto God in every place (II Cor. II, 1).
